Vector and tensor spin polarization for vector Bosons at local equilibrium
Zhong-Hua Zhang, Xu-Guang Huang, Francesco Becattini, Xin-Li Sheng

Abstract
We derive expressions for the vector and tensor components of the spin polarization of massive vector bosons at local thermodynamic equilibrium up to second order in the space-time gradients of the thermodynamic fields pertaining to the canonical stress-energy tensor and spin tensor of the free Proca field. A set of Feynman rules is devised to calculate the Wigner function and the matrix-valued spin-dependent distribution (MVSD) functions order by order in space-time gradients. Due to constraints imposed by time-reversal symmetry, the leading contribution to spin alignment - defined as the 00-component of the tensor polarization - arises from second-order terms in MVSD, for which we provide an analytic formula. We discuss the physical meaning of different contributions to vector and tensor polarization. These formulae provide a prediction of a contribution to the spin alignment which can be compared with the observations in relativistic heavy-ion collisions.



arXiv:
2412.19416

1
Introduction

Spin polarization is a ubiquitous topic across several fields in physics. In atomic
physics and condensed matter physics, spin has been a frontier research subject for a long time.
In cosmology, the B-mode polarization of the cosmic microwave background photons can be
used to test the existence of primordial gravitational waves
BICEP2:2014owc
;
BICEP:2021xfz
,
as well as to elucidate the nature of dark matter and dark energy
Komatsu:2022nvu
.
In particle physics, the proton spin problem has triggered extensive discussions that have
greatly advanced our understanding of Quantum Chromo-Dynamics
Aidala:2012mv
;
Ji:2020ena
.

Lately, spin has become an important observable in Quark-Gluon-Plasma physics. Following
early predictions based on partonic angular momentum transfer in relativistic heavy-ion collisions
(HICs)
Liang:2004ph
;
Voloshin:2004ha
;
Gao:2007bc
;
Huang:2011ru
and on the quantitative
predictions based on statistical local equilibrium
Becattini:2013fla
;
Fang:2016vpj
;
Liu:2020flb
,
the experiment of the STAR collaboration found evidence of global spin polarization of
\Lambda
(and
\bar{\Lambda}
)
STAR:2017ckg
;
STAR:2018gyt
;
STAR:2021beb
,

\Xi^{-}
, and
\Omega^{-}
hyperons
STAR:2020xbm
supporting the predictions based on
hydrodynamic and statistical local equilibrium. However, these predictions were
not confirmed in the study of local polarization, that is spin polarization as a function of momentum
Becattini:2017gcx
;
Wei:2018zfb
;
Florkowski:2019voj
;
Wu:2019eyi
;
Xie:2019jun
;
Xia:2019fjf
;
Becattini:2019ntv
;
Liu:2019krs
;
Fu:2020oxj
;
STAR:2019erd
;
ALICE:2021pzu
. It was later
found out that further terms which are linear in the gradients of the thermo-hydrodynamic fields
are responsible for spin polarization at local equilibrium (spin-thermal shear and spin
Hall effect) which can reproduce the local polarization data;
for recent reviews, see
Liu:2020ymh
;
Becattini:2020ngo
;
Gao:2020vbh
;
Huang:2020dtn
;
Jian-Hua:2023cna
;
Niida:2024ntm
;
Becattini:2024uha
.

Similar spin polarization phenomena could occur in HICs for vector mesons
Liang:2004xn
.
In this case, the benchmark quantity is the so-called spin alignment parameter,
\Theta_{00}-1/3
.
Here,
\Theta_{00}
is the
00
-element of the spin density matrix of a vector meson, representing
the probability of the vector meson being in the state with spin-0 in its rest frame in a specified quantization direction, i.e.the longitudinally polarized mode (see section
2

for discussions of the spin state of vector bosons). Spin alignment is essentially a component of vector meson’s tensor polarization, which will be discussed in detail in section
2
.

Recently, the STAR Collaboration reported the measurements of the global spin alignment of

\phi(1020)
and
K^{*0}(892)

mesons
STAR:2022fan
, i.e., the average spin alignment with respect to the direction of the
global orbital angular momentum (OAM) of the colliding system (which coincides with the direction
of the reaction plane for symmetric collisions such as Au + Au). They found that

\Theta_{00}-1/3>0
for
\phi
meson and
\Theta_{00}-1/3\approx 0
for
K^{*0}
meson
at BNL Relativistic Heavy Ion Collider (RHIC) energies. On the other hand, the ALICE Collaboration reported that
\Theta_{00}-1/3<0

for both
\phi
and
K^{*0}
in the low-
p_{T}
region at the LHC energy
ALICE:2019aid
.
A substantial spin alignment of
J/\psi(3097)
meson with
\Theta_{00}-1/3<0
at low-
p_{T}

has also been observed by the ALICE Collaboration
ALICE:2020iev
;
ALICE:2022dyy
.

Spin alignment is a challenging puzzle in HICs because the results depend strongly on both collision energies and meson type
STAR:2022fan
.
Moreover, its magnitude for
\phi
meson is large (
\mathcal{O}(10^{-2})
), which is at variance with extrapolations from the
hydrodynamic-local equilibrium model and other models proposed in the literature.
For instance, in a quark coalescence model, supposing that quarks coalesce to form mesons and that their spins are at local equilibrium at the time of hadronization, one may naively expect that the spin alignment of vector mesons would be of the order of
P_{q}^{2}
with
P_{q}
the spin polarization of quarks and antiquarks.
However, this is of the order of
\mathcal{O}(10^{-4})
assuming that
P_{q}
is about the same
magnitude of the global spin polarization of
\Lambda

STAR:2017ckg
. In view of these failures,
theorists have proposed various mechanisms beyond local equilibrium calculations, such as contributions
from electromagnetic field
Yang:2017sdk
;
Sheng:2019kmk
;
Xia:2020tyd
;
Sheng:2022ssp
;
Li:2023tsf
;
Zhao:2024ipr
, strong
\phi
mean field
Sheng:2019kmk
;
Sheng:2022wsy
;
Sheng:2023urn
, medium modification to meson spectra
Li:2022vmb
;
Wei:2023pdf
;
Fu:2023qht
;
Sun:2024anu
;
Li:2024qae
, and gluonic color fields
Kumar:2022ylt
;
Kumar:2023ghs
, based on various theoretical approaches such as quantum kinetic theories
Yang:2021fea
;
Sheng:2022ffb
;
Wagner:2022gza
;
Kumar:2023ghs
;
Kumar:2023ojl
;
Yin:2024dnu
, effective models
Sheng:2022ssp
;
Wei:2023pdf
;
Sun:2024anu
, linear response theories
Li:2022vmb
;
Dong:2023cng
, and holographic models
Zhao:2024ipr
;
Sheng:2024kgg
.

In a quantum hydrodynamic picture, a full calculation of spin alignment
has not been carried out yet. An estimate in ref.
Sheng:2019kmk
shows that the contribution to
\Theta_{00}-1/3
from B-part (E-part) of fluid vorticity is around
\mathcal{O}(10^{-5})
(
\mathcal{O}(10^{-4})
). But it was based on a quark coalescence scenario with quarks polarized by the vorticity field without incorporating the direct coupling between meson’s spin and vorticity. On the other hand, most of the aforementioned hydrodynamic estimates rely on formulae
that apply to global equilibrium, where the thermal shear tensor vanishes. At local thermodynamic
equilibrium, the contribution from thermal shear tensor to vector meson spin alignment, unlike to fermion spin polarization
Becattini:2021suc
;
Liu:2021uhn
, also vanishes at the linear order, as will be shown later. Nevertheless, there might be a non-vanishing dissipative contribution at the linear order in the thermal
shear; a particular calculation has been carried out in ref.
Li:2022vmb
which, however, shows
a critical dependence on the lifetime of the vector meson. This was confirmed in the framework of relativistic
kinetic theory
Wagner:2022gza
;
Dong:2023cng
.

In this article, we use quantum statistical methods, notably the Local Equilibrium Density Operator (LEDO)
Zubarev:1979
;
Weert:1982
;
Becattini:2019dxo
, to derive a formula (“Cooper-Frye type”) for the vector and
tensor spin polarization of vector bosons at local equilibrium, which is complete up to the second order
in space-time gradients. Quantum statistical methods take into account the effects of non-constant
thermodynamic fields in a very effective way and, particularly, they show that even local equilibrium
involves gradient corrections to the distribution functions. This method has been used to derive the
spin-thermal shear coupling
Becattini:2021suc
;
Buzzegoli:2022fxu
and the mean spin
polarization vector for spin-1/2 fermions at both first order
Liu:2021nyg
;
Buzzegoli:2021wlg
and
second order
Sheng:2024pbw
in space-time gradients. The second-order gradient corrections we report
in this work most likely include those found in ref.
Kumar:2023ojl
in that they are obtained by expanding
the local equilibrium density operator.
We note that, even though we identify gradients of the thermal vorticity or thermal shear tensors
\partial\varpi
and
\partial\xi
as the second-order terms in our power counting scheme, their magnitude could be large due to significant spatial variation of
\varpi
and
\xi
around the freeze-out hypersurface. Their contributions to the spin alignment could be important in realistic heavy-ion collisions, which need to be investigated in numerical simulations.

It should be emphasized that, as the calculation is at local
equilibrium, its result depends on the specific form of the stress-energy tensor and spin tensor, the
so-called pseudo-gauge choice
Becattini:2018duy
. In this work, we focus on the canonical stress-energy
and spin tensors obtained from the familiar Proca Lagrangian (see eq. (
1
)) and we
allow a spin potential
\Omega
differing from thermal vorticity
\varpi
. It should be pointed out
that the corresponding expressions in the Belinfante pseudo gauge cannot be obtained from our results
by simply setting
\Omega=\varpi
.

The paper is organized as follows.
In section
2
, we briefly discuss the definition of the Matrix-Valued Spin-dependent Distribution (MVSD) and the description of the spin state of massive bosons using the spin density matrix.
In section
3
, we introduce LEDO and its cumulant expansion based on our power counting
rules which will be given accordingly.
In section
4
, we show how to extract the vector and tensor polarization from the MVSD.
The zeroth- and first-order results of the MVSD are given in section
5
.
In section
6
, we introduce a Feynman diagram scheme and apply it to the calculation
of the second-order terms.
The second-order MVSD is given in section
7
with diagrammatic representations.
In section
, we take the global equilibrium limit and obtain the spin alignment at global equilibrium. We also derive a new contribution from the space-time gradients of the thermal vorticity beyond global equilibrium.
We summarize our results and give an outlook in section
.

Throughout this paper, we use the Minkowski metric
\eta_{\mu\nu}={\rm{diag}}(1,-1,-1,-1)
and adopt natural units
k_{B}=c=\hbar=1
. The Levi-Civita tensor
\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}
is normalized as
\epsilon^{0123}=-\epsilon_{0123}=1
. A bigger hat over a symbol, such as
\widehat{O}
, denotes an operator in Hilbert space. A smaller hat, such as
\hat{k}
, denotes a suitably normalized four-vector, for example,
\hat{k}^{\mu}=k^{\mu}/({\hat{t}}\cdot k)
with
{\hat{t}}^{\mu}=(1,{\bm{0}})
being the normal vector in the time direction. We also define the shorthand notations
A^{[\mu}B^{\nu]}=A^{\mu}B^{\nu}-A^{\nu}B^{\mu}
and
A^{(\mu}B^{\nu)}=A^{\mu}B^{\nu}+A^{\nu}B^{\mu}
for arbitrary two vectors
A^{\mu}
and
B^{\mu}
.

2
Spin state of a massive vector boson

We consider free neutral massive vector bosons, such as
\phi
and
J/\psi
, which are described by the Proca Lagrangian

{\cal L}=-\frac{1}{4}F_{\mu\nu}F^{\mu\nu}+\frac{1}{2}m^{2}A_{\mu}A^{\mu}

(1)

with
m
being the boson’s mass and the field tensor

F^{\mu\nu}\equiv\partial^{\mu}A^{\nu}-\partial^{\nu}A^{\mu}.

The equations of motion (EOMs) for massive vector field
A^{\mu}
obtained from eq. (
1
) are given by

\displaystyle(\partial^{2}+m^{2})A^{\mu}=0\,,

(2)

\displaystyle\partial_{\mu}A^{\mu}=0\,,

(3)

where the second equation is the Lorenz condition for the massive vector field.

The mode decomposition of the vector field
A^{\mu}
after quantization reads,

\widehat{A}^{\mu}(x)=\sum_{s}\int\frac{d^{3}{\bm{k}}}{(2\pi)^{3}}\frac{1}{2E_{\bm{k}}}\left[\widehat{a}_{\bm{k}}^{s}\epsilon^{\mu}_{s}(k)e^{-ik\cdot x}+\widehat{a}_{\bm{k}}^{s{\dagger}}\epsilon_{s}^{\mu*}(k)e^{ik\cdot x}\right],

(4)

where
k^{0}=E_{\bm{k}}=\sqrt{{\bm{k}}^{2}+m^{2}}
,
\epsilon_{s}^{\mu}(p)
is the polarization vectors
1
1

1

Note that
\epsilon_{s}^{\mu}(p)
depends only on
{\bm{p}}
, but for later use, it is convenient to consider
E_{\bm{p}}
in
\epsilon_{s}^{\mu}(p)
as
p^{0}
.
, and
\widehat{a}_{\bm{k}}^{s}
and
\widehat{a}_{\bm{k}}^{s{\dagger}}
are annihilation and creation operators for vector boson of momentum
{\bm{k}}
and spin state
s
. They satisfy the commutation relation

[\widehat{a}_{\bm{k}}^{s},\widehat{a}_{{\bm{k}}^{\prime}}^{s^{\prime}{\dagger}}]=(2E_{\bm{k}})(2\pi)^{3}\delta^{(3)}({\bm{k}}-{\bm{k}}^{\prime})\delta^{ss^{\prime}}.

(5)

The decomposition in eq. (
4
) can be straightforwardly extended to the case of
charged vector bosons (such as
K^{*0}
and
D^{*+}
mesons) by introducing different operators
for particles and antiparticles.

The physical meaning of
s
, which is a quantum number taking values
-1,0,1
depends on the choice
of the so-called
standard Lorentz transformation

[k]
, which is defined as the Lorentz transformation
turning the standard vector
k_{0}^{\mu}=(m,{\bf 0})
of a particle with mass
m
into its actual
four-momentum
k
. As it is well known, if
[k]=R_{z}(\phi)R_{y}(\theta)L_{z}(\xi)
where
\phi,\theta,\xi

are the polar and hyperbolic coordinates of the four-vector
p
,
R_{i}
are rotations around the
axis
i=x,y,z
and
L_{i}
boosts along axis
i
,
s
is the helicity of the particle.
On the other hand, if
[k]
is chosen to be a pure Lorentz boost,
s
can be thought of as a spin
component along the boosted quantization axis. It is important to stress that both the creation and
annihilation operators as well as the polarization vectors
\epsilon_{s}
depend on the choice of the standard Lorentz transformation,
so that, strictly, one should write

\epsilon^{\mu}_{s}([k])\hskip 20.00003pt\hskip 20.00003pt\widehat{a}^{s}([k])

even though their combination, summed over
s
, in the field expansion is altogether independent
thereof. Throughout this paper, this dependence will not be explicitly shown in the notation, and
we will keep indicating
\widehat{a}_{\bm{k}}^{s}
and
\epsilon^{\mu}_{s}(k)
. Of course, if
k^{\mu}=k^{\mu}_{0}
, there is no
dependence on the standard Lorentz transformation, which is the identity in all cases.

The Lorentz condition in eq. (
3
) enforces the polarization four-vectors to satisfy

k_{\mu}\epsilon_{s}^{\mu}(k)=0
, and such a condition ensures that
\epsilon^{\mu}_{s}(k)
in boson’s rest frame has
a vanishing time component. Therefore, there are only three linearly independent
\epsilon^{\mu}_{s}(k_{0})

vectors, which can be chosen as the basis vectors of the spin-1 representation space,
with, e.g., the
y
axis as the spin quantization direction. In this case, these vectors are the
eigenvectors of the representation matrix of the generator
J_{y}
, that is

\begin{cases}\displaystyle{\bm{e}}_{1}=-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(i,0,1)\,,\\
\displaystyle{\bm{e}}_{0}=(0,1,0)\,,\\
\displaystyle{\bm{e}}_{-1}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(-i,0,1)\,.\end{cases}

The polarization four-vectors are then
\epsilon^{\mu}_{s}(k_{0})=(0,{\bm{e}}_{s})
and the
\epsilon^{\mu}_{s}(k)
can be
obtained by applying the standard Lorentz transformation
[k]
, that is
\epsilon^{\mu}_{s}(k)=[k]^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu}}}\epsilon^{\nu}_{s}(k_{0})
.
Furthermore, one can also introduce the “scalar” polarization vector as
\epsilon^{\mu}_{o}(k_{0})={\hat{t}}^{\mu}

in the rest frame, with
{\hat{t}}^{\mu}=(1,\bm{0})
.
Thereby, the four four-vectors
\epsilon^{\mu}_{1}
,
\epsilon^{\mu}_{0}
,
\epsilon^{\mu}_{-1}
,
\epsilon^{\mu}_{o}
at either
k_{0}
or
k

form a
vierbein
that satisfies the following orthonormality and completeness relations

\eta^{ab}\epsilon_{a}^{\mu}\epsilon_{b}^{\nu*}=\eta^{\mu\nu},\hskip 10.00002pt\eta_{\mu\nu}\epsilon^{\mu}_{a}\epsilon^{\nu*}_{b}=\eta_{ab}\,,

(6)

where
a,b
take the values from
o,0,\pm 1
,
\eta^{ab}
and
\eta_{ab}
are defined
as

\eta_{ab}=\eta^{ab}=\begin{cases}1&a=b=o,\\
-1&a=b=0,\pm 1\\
0&\text{else}.\end{cases}

(7)

For a reason that will become clear below, it is convenient to choose
[k]
as a pure boost
L(k)

carrying the four-momentum
k^{\mu}_{0}
to
k^{\mu}
, so that
\epsilon_{s}^{\mu}(k)=L^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu}}}(k)\epsilon^{\nu}_{s}(k_{0})

with

L^{0}_{~0}(k)=E_{\bm{k}}/m\,,\hskip 20.00003ptL^{0}_{~i}(k)=L^{i}_{~0}(k)=k^{i}/m\,,\hskip 20.00003ptL^{i}_{~j}(k)=\delta^{i}_{j}-k^{i}k_{j}/[m(E_{\bm{k}}+m)]\,.

(8)

Therefore,

\epsilon_{s}^{\mu}(k)=\left(\frac{{\bm{k}}\cdot{\bm{e}}_{s}}{m}\,,\,{\bm{e}}_{s}+\frac{{\bm{k}}\cdot{\bm{e}}_{s}}{m(E_{\bm{k}}+m)}{\bm{k}}\right)\,,\hskip 10.00002pts=\pm 1,0\,.

(9)

or, in a manifestly covariant form

\epsilon_{s}^{\mu}(k)=\epsilon^{\mu}_{s}(k_{0})-\frac{k\cdot\epsilon_{s}(k_{0})}{m({\hat{t}}\cdot k+m)}(k^{\mu}+m{\hat{t}}^{\mu})\,,\hskip 10.00002pts=\pm 1,0\,.

(10)

Since the
\epsilon_{s}^{\mu}(k)
are perpendicular to the four-vector
k^{\mu}
, the polarization vectors

\epsilon_{s}^{\mu}(k),\ s=0,\pm 1
form a set of a complete basis orthogonal to
k^{\mu}
(which can also be obtained from the first relation in eq. (
6
)),

\sum_{s=-1}^{+1}\epsilon_{s}^{\mu}(k)\epsilon_{s}^{\nu*}(k)=-\eta^{\mu\nu}+\frac{k^{\mu}k^{\nu}}{m^{2}}\,.

(11)

The spin polarization state of a relativistic particle is described by the so-called
spin density matrix. In a relativistic quantum field theory, this matrix can be defined
as
Becattini:2020sww

\Theta_{rs}(k)=\frac{\Tr\left(\widehat{\rho}\,\widehat{a}^{s\dagger}_{{\bm{k}}}\widehat{a}^{r}_{{\bm{k}}}\right)}{\sum_{r}\Tr\left(\widehat{\rho}\,\widehat{a}^{r\dagger}_{{\bm{k}}}\widehat{a}^{r}_{{\bm{k}}}\right)}\,,

(12)

where
r,s
are the spin-state indices running from
-S
to
S
with
S
the particle spin and

k
is the four-momentum of the particle in a conventional global frame (e.g. the laboratory frame).
The spin density matrix can be used to express all measurable quantities related to the spin of
the particle. However, the spin density matrix, featuring a quadratic combination of creation
and annihilation operators, also depends on the choice of the standard Lorentz transformation.
Notably, it can be shown (see appendix
) that if one defined it by using a
different standard Lorentz transformation
[k]^{\prime}
, then the new spin density matrix would be

\Theta^{\prime}(k)_{tu}=\sum_{rs}D^{S}(R^{-1})_{tr}\Theta(k)_{rs}D^{S}(R)_{su}\,,

where
R\equiv[k]^{-1}[k]^{\prime}
is a rotation as it keeps the time direction unchanged,
and
D^{S}
stands for the spin-
S
irreducible representation of the SO(3) group.

Even though the spin density matrix depends on the standard Lorentz transformation, measurable
physical quantities must be independent thereof. For instance, it can be shown that the spin
polarization four-vector in the conventional frame (i.e. the frame where
the four-momentum of the particle is
k
), defined as the mean value of the Pauli-Lubanski
vector reads
Becattini:2020sww

S^{\mu}(k)=\sum_{i=1}^{3}\tr\left(D^{S}(J^{i})\Theta(k)\right)[k]^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{i}}}\,,

(13)

where
J^{i}
are the generators of the rotation group (also known as angular momenta)
and
D^{S}(J^{i})
their associated matrices in the representation of spin-
S
. The four-vector
S^{\mu}(k)
in
eq. (
13
) turns out to be independent of the choice of
[k]
(see appendix
),
hence
S^{\mu}(k)
is that of an actual physical vector.
To determine the spin polarization vector in the rest frame of the particle, which
is the measurable quantity, a Lorentz transformation is to be carried out. This
is usually done keeping in the rest frame the same space axes of the conventional frame:
otherwise stated with a pure Lorentz boost
L(k)^{-1}
. Looking at eq. (
13
),
it can be then concluded that the spin polarization vector in the rest frame can be
expressed as

S^{i}_{\text{rest}}(k)=\tr\left(D^{S}(J^{i})\Theta(k)\right)\,,

(14)

(with the time component vanishes) only if the standard Lorentz transformation
[k]
in
eq. (
13
) is a pure boost
L(k)
. Note that in eq. (
14
), the
spin vector components are in the rest frame of the particle, but its argument
k
is the
four-momentum in the conventional observer frame.

Similar arguments apply to all other physical quantities that can be formed out of the
spin density matrix, like tensors. For the spin-1 case, being
\Theta(k)
a
3\times 3
Hermitian
matrix with unitary trace, it can be decomposed as follows

\Theta(k)=\frac{1}{3}I+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{3}S^{i}_{\text{rest}}(k)S^{i}+\frac{1}{4}\sum_{i,j=1}^{3}{\mathfrak{T}}^{ij}(k)\Sigma^{ij}\,,

(15)

where
S^{i}\equiv D^{1}(J^{i})
(fulfilling
[S^{i},S^{j}]=i\sum_{k}\epsilon^{ijk}S^{k}
),
\bm{S}^{2}=2I
and

\Sigma^{ij}=S^{i}S^{j}+S^{j}S^{i}-\frac{2}{3}\bm{S}^{2}\delta^{ij}
.
Choosing
\hat{\bm{y}}
as the spin quantization axis, one has

S^{1}=S_{x}=\frac{i}{\sqrt{2}}\begin{pmatrix}0&-1&0\\
1&0&-1\\
0&1&0\end{pmatrix},\hskip 10.00002ptS^{2}=S_{y}=\begin{pmatrix}1&0&0\\
0&0&0\\
0&0&-1\end{pmatrix},\hskip 10.00002ptS^{3}=S_{z}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\begin{pmatrix}0&1&0\\
1&0&1\\
0&1&0\end{pmatrix}\,.

(16)

Since

\displaystyle\tr S^{i}=\tr\Sigma^{ij}=0\hskip 20.00003pt\hskip 20.00003pt\tr(S^i S^j)=2\delta^{ij}

\displaystyle\tr(S^i \S^{jk})=0\hskip 20.00003pt\hskip 20.00003pt\tr(\S^{ij} \S^{lk})=2(\delta^{il}\delta^{jk}+\delta^{ik}\delta^{jl})\,,

one can readily invert the above formulae

\displaystyle S^{i}_{\text{rest}}(k)

\displaystyle=\tr\left(S^{i}\Theta(k)\right)\,,

(17)

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{T}}^{ij}(k)

\displaystyle=\tr\left(\Sigma^{ij}\Theta(k)\right)\,.

(18)

Therefore, comparing with eq. (
14
) and recalling the discussion
of its meaning, it turns out that the
S_{\text{rest}}^{i}(k)
in eq. (
15
)
(in accordance with the chosen notation) are just the components of the spin vector
in the rest frame of the particle, provided that the standard Lorentz transformation is a pure
boost. Similarly, the proper physical polarization tensor should be defined in the frame
where the particle four-momentum is
k
, and it turns out to be

{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mu\nu}(k)=\sum_{ij}\tr(\Sigma^{ij} \Theta(k))~[k]^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{i}}}~[k]^{{{\nu}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{j}}}\,,

which is independent of the standard Lorentz transformation.
Equation (
18
) provides the components of the same tensor in the particle rest frame only if the standard
Lorentz transformation is a pure Lorentz boost.

Instead of the Cartesian components of the above operators, we can use their spherical irreducible
(under the rotation group SO(3)) components. Denoting
S_{1,m}
and
S_{2,m}
the spherical
components of
\bm{S}
, that is
S_{1,0}=S_{y},S_{1,\pm 1}=\mp(S_{z}\pm iS_{x})/\sqrt{2}
and

S_{2,m}=\sum_{m_{1},m_{2}=-1}^{1}\innerproduct{1,m_1;1,m_2}{2,m}S_{1,m_{1}}S_{1,m_{2}}\,,

(19)

where
\innerproduct{1,m_1;1,m_2}{2,m}
are the Clebsch-Gordon coefficients; and further denoting

P_{1,m}(k)=\tr\left\{S_{1,m}\Theta(k)\right\}\hskip 20.00003pt\hskip 20.00003pt{\mathfrak{T}}_{2,m}(k)=\tr\left\{S_{2,m}\Theta(k)\right\}

(20)

the relevant traces of the spin density matrix, one has

\Theta(k)=\frac{1}{3}I+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{m=-1}^{1}(-1)^{m}P_{1,-m}(k)S_{1,m}+\sum_{m=-2}^{2}(-1)^{m}{\mathfrak{T}}_{2,-m}(k)S_{2,m}.\;\;;

(21)

for more details, see ref.
Sakurai:2011zz
. After lengthy calculations, one can show that

\Theta_{00}-1/3=-\sqrt{2/3}{\mathfrak{T}}_{2,0}

which is, by definition, the
spin alignment
parameter of the vector boson associated
with a given spin quantization direction (here,
\hat{{\bm{y}}}
).

The irreducible tensor
{\mathfrak{T}}_{2,m}
plays a major role in the decay of polarized vector
mesons. For example, in the decay
\phi\rightarrow K^{+}+K^{-}
, this tensor determines
the angular distribution
(1/N)(dN/d\Omega)(\theta,\phi)
of either daughter particle.
Due to the transformation property under rotation, the angular distribution must have the following form

\frac{1}{N}\frac{dN}{d\Omega}(\theta,\phi)=\frac{1}{4\pi}+\alpha\sum_{m=-1}^{1}(-1)^{m}P_{1,m}Y_{1,-m}(\theta,\phi)+\beta\sum_{m=-2}^{2}(-1)^{m}{\mathfrak{T}}_{2,m}Y_{2,-m}(\theta,\phi)\,,

(22)

where
(\theta,\phi)
are the polar and azimuthal angles with respect to the spin quantization
axis in the rest frame of the mother particle and
Y_{l,m}(\theta,\phi)
the spherical harmonic
functions. One can show that
\alpha=0
for strong decays because of the pairty conservation,
and
\beta=-\sqrt{\frac{3}{10\pi}}
for pseudoscalar meson productions, which is obtained after
explicit calculation using the Wigner D-matrix formalism
Schilling:1969um
;
Yang:2017sdk
.
Therefore, only the tensor polarization
{\mathfrak{T}}_{2,m}
can be measured in the pseudo-scalar
meson decay modes of the vector mesons. If we integrate over the azimuthal angle, we have

\frac{1}{N}\frac{dN}{d\cos{\h}}=\frac{1}{4\pi}-\frac{1}{4\pi}(3\cos^{2}{\theta}-1)\sqrt{\frac{3}{2}}{\mathfrak{T}}_{2,0}.

(23)

Thus,
\Theta_{00}-1/3=-\sqrt{2/3}{\mathfrak{T}}_{2,0}
is the only parameter that determines the polar angle distribution of the decay daughters in the rest frame of the vector meson.

For the purpose of the calculations of polarization in a relativistic fluid, it is very useful
to introduce the Matrix-Valued Spin-dependent Distribution (MVSD) operator

\widehat{f}_{rs}(x,k)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\epsilon_{\mu}^{r*}(k)\epsilon_{\nu}^{s}(k)\widehat{W}_{+}^{\mu\nu}(x,k)

(24)

with
\widehat{W}_{+}^{\mu\nu}(x,k)
standing for the positive-energy (particle) part of the Wigner operator

\widehat{W}_{+}^{\mu\nu}(x,k)=\int d^{4}y\,e^{ik\cdot y}\widehat{A}^{\nu}\left(x-\frac{y}{2}\right)\widehat{A}^{\mu}\left(x+\frac{y}{2}\right)\theta(k^{2})\theta(k^{0})\,,

(25)

where
\theta
is the Heaviside step function.
With eq. (
4
), we obtain

\begin{split}\widehat{W}_{+}^{\mu\nu}(x,k)=&~\int\frac{d^{\,3}{{\bm{p}}_{1}}}{(2\pi)^{3}}\frac{d^{\,3}{{\bm{p}}_{2}}}{(2\pi)^{3}}\frac{1}{2E_{{\bm{p}}_{1}}}\frac{1}{2E_{{\bm{p}}_{2}}}(2\pi)^{4}\sum_{a_{1}a_{2}}e^{-i(p_{1}-p_{2})\cdot x}\\
&~\times\delta^{4}\left(k-\frac{p_{1}}{2}-\frac{p_{2}}{2}\right)\widehat{a}^{a_{2}\dagger}_{{\bm{p}}_{2}}\widehat{a}^{a_{1}}_{{\bm{p}}_{1}}\epsilon^{\mu}_{a_{1}}(p_{1})\epsilon^{\nu*}_{a_{2}}(p_{2})\,.\\
\end{split}

(26)

In general, the argument
k
is not on the mass shell in eq. (
24
) and eq. (
26
). However, since

p_{1}^{\mu}
and
p_{2}^{\mu}
lie on the mass shell, we have
(p_{1}-p_{2})\cdot(p_{1}+p_{2})=0
. Then, the
Wigner operator fulfills the equation
(k\cdot\partial_{x})\widehat{W}_{+}^{\mu\nu}(x,k)=0

Becattini:2020sww
.
Likewise, from the eq. (
24
), we have

\left(k\cdot\partial_{x}\right)\widehat{f}_{rs}(x,k)=0\,.

(27)

It can be shown in ref.
Becattini:2020sww
that this equation makes the argument
k
an on-shell
four-vector
after
integration over an arbitrary 3D hypersurface
\Sigma
with suitable
boundary conditions. From eqs. (
24
), (
26
), and (
12
) it can be shown that

\int_{\Sigma}d\Sigma\cdot k\;\Tr(\opt{\r}\,\opt{f}_{rs}(x,k))=\frac{1}{2}\delta(k^{2}-m^{2})\theta(k^{0})\Tr\left(\widehat{\rho}\,\widehat{a}^{s\dagger}_{{\bm{k}}}\widehat{a}^{r}_{{\bm{k}}}\right)\,,

so that the spin density matrix in eq. (
12
) can be expressed as
an integral over an arbitrary 3D hypersurface
\Sigma

\Theta_{rs}(k)=\frac{\int_{\Sigma}d\Sigma\cdot k\;\Tr(\opt{\r}\,\opt{f}_{rs}(x,k))}{\sum_{r}\int_{\Sigma}d\Sigma\cdot k\;\Tr(\opt{\r}\,\opt{f}_{rr}(x,k))}\,.

(28)

eq. (
27
) ensures that the four-vector
k
is on-shell, i.e.
k^{2}=m^{2}
.
eq. (
28
) is a very useful form for the applications to relativistic
hydrodynamics because the MVSD, unlike the spin density matrix overall, depends on
x
and its
value is mostly determined by values of hydrodynamic fields around
x
at local thermodynamic equilibrium.

It is also possible to parametrize the MVSD as the product

f_{rs}(x,k)=\Theta_{rs}(x,k)f(x,k)\,,

(29)

where
f(x,k)=\sum_{r}f_{rr}(x,k)
is the trace of the MSVD, so that
\tr\Theta(x,k)=1
.
The function
f(x,k)
will be henceforth denoted as scalar distribution and
\Theta_{rs}(x,k)

as the spin density matrix in the phase space.

3
Local equilibrium density operator

From the free Lagrangian for a neutral spin-1 particle eq. (
1
), one can derive the canonical
form of the energy-momentum and spin tensors

\displaystyle\widehat{T}^{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=-\widehat{F}^{\mu\rho}\partial^{\nu}\widehat{A}_{\rho}-\eta^{\mu\nu}\left(-\frac{1}{4}\widehat{F}_{\rho\sigma}\widehat{F}^{\rho\sigma}+\frac{1}{2}m^{2}\widehat{A}_{\rho}\widehat{A}^{\rho}\right)\,,

(30)

\displaystyle\widehat{S}^{\mu\rho\sigma}

\displaystyle=-\widehat{F}^{\mu\rho}\widehat{A}^{\sigma}+\widehat{F}^{\mu\sigma}\widehat{A}^{\rho}\,.

(31)

The corresponding local equilibrium density operator (LEDO) describing a local Gibbs state specified by the thermal velocity
\beta_{\mu}(x)
and spin potential
\Omega_{\rho\sigma}(x)
is given by
Zubarev:1979
;
Weert:1982
;
Becattini:2019dxo

\widehat{\rho}_{\rm LE}=\frac{1}{Z_{\rm LE}}\exp\{-\int_{\Sigma}d\Sigma_{\mu}(y)\left[\widehat{T}^{\mu\nu}(y)\beta_{\nu}(y)-\frac{1}{2}\widehat{S}^{\mu\rho\sigma}(y)\Omega_{\rho\sigma}(y)\right]\bigg\}\,,

(32)

where
d\Sigma_{\mu}
is the shorthand notation for
d\Sigma~n_{\mu}
with
d\Sigma
being the measure of the hypersurface
\Sigma
and
n_{\mu}
being its normal vector.
Z_{\rm LE}
is the local equilibrium partition function, which is the trace of the exponent term and ensures that the LEDO is properly normalized,
\Tr\widehat{\rho}=1
.

Figure 1:
A sketch of the space-time diagram of a relativistic heavy ion collision.
\Sigma_{{\rm LE}}
is the 3D space-like hypersurface where the local thermal equilibrium is achieved.
\Sigma_{{\rm FO}}
is the freeze-out hypersurface, consisting of space-like hypersurface
\Xi
and time-like hypersurface
\Sigma_{t}
. In
\Omega
, the volume surrounded by
\Sigma_{{\rm LE}}
and
\Sigma_{{\rm FO}}
, the matter is in the QGP phase.
\Xi_{B}
is the hyperplane connecting the boundary of
\Sigma_{{\rm FO}}
.

Note that, in the LEDO, we treat the spin potential as an independent parameter. This is in line with the perspective of spin hydrodynamics
Florkowski:2017ruc
;
Hattori:2019lfp
;
Fukushima:2020ucl
;
Hongo:2021ona
;
Cao:2022aku
;
She:2021lhe
;
Gallegos:2021bzp
(for a review of spin hydrodynamics, see ref.
Huang:2024ffg
), where the spin polarization density relaxes to local equilibrium comparably slower
than momentum degrees of freedom
Hattori:2019lfp
;
Hongo:2021ona
;
Becattini:2018duy
. The
spin potential will affect the spin alignment of vector bosons, as we will show.

The coupling field
\beta_{\nu}(x),\,\Omega_{\rho\sigma}(x)
should be understood as Lagrange multipliers
for the maximization of entropy

S[\Sigma]=-\Tr(\opt{\r}\ln{\opt{\r}})\,,

under the constraints

\displaystyle n_{\mu}T^{\mu\nu}(x)

\displaystyle=n_{\mu}\Tr\left\{\widehat{T}^{\mu\nu}(x)\widehat{\rho}_{\rm LE}[\beta,\Omega]\right\}\,,

(33)

\displaystyle n_{\mu}S^{\mu\rho\sigma}(x)

\displaystyle=n_{\mu}\Tr\left\{\widehat{S}^{\mu\rho\sigma}(x)\widehat{\rho}_{\rm LE}[\beta,\Omega]\right\}\,,

(34)

where, on the left-hand side, there are the actual renormalized values of the currents.

The actual density operator in the Heisenberg picture must be static and
\widehat{\rho}_{\rm LE}
,
in general, is not. Nevertheless, the actual density operator of a system that achieves, at
some time, local thermodynamic equilibrium can be expressed as the LEDO for some
fixed
hypersurface (
\Sigma_{LE}
in figure
1
).
By means of the Gauss theorem, the density operator can be rewritten as an exponential
of the 3D integral over the hypersurface at some “current” time plus a 4D integral over
the encompassed region between the two 3D integrations
Becattini:2019dxo
.
Provided that the thermodynamic fields
\beta
and
\Omega
are slowly varying, the
4D integral, which represents the dissipative contribution to any observable, can be
neglected and one is left with the local equilibrium density operator at the current time;
for relativistic heavy ion collisions, this is the freeze-out hypersurface
\Sigma_{FO}
.
Therefore, in practice, if the dissipative part is neglected, the calculation of mean
values can be carried out at the local thermodynamic equilibrium at the freeze-out by
replacing the hypersurface
\Sigma
with
\Sigma_{FO}
in eq. (
32
).

In view of the above discussion, for a local Heisenberg operator
\widehat{O}(x)
, such as

\widehat{f}_{rs}(x,k)
, we have its ensemble average given by

O(x)\equiv\left<\widehat{O}(x)\right>\equiv\Tr[\widehat{\rho}_{\rm LE}\widehat{O}(x)]=\frac{1}{Z_{\rm LE}}\Tr\left[e^{\widehat{A}+\widehat{B}}\widehat{O}(x)\right],

(35)

where we have introduced the abbreviations

\displaystyle\widehat{A}

\displaystyle=-\widehat{P}^{\mu}\beta_{\mu}(x)\,,

(36)

\displaystyle\widehat{B}

\displaystyle=-\int_{\Sigma_{{\rm FO}}}d\Sigma_{\mu}(y)\left(\widehat{T}^{\mu\nu}(y)\Delta\beta_{\nu}(y)-\frac{1}{2}\widehat{S}^{\mu\rho\sigma}(y)\Omega_{\rho\sigma}(y)\right)

(37)

with
\widehat{P}^{\mu}=\int_{\Sigma_{{\rm FO}}}d\Sigma_{\nu}(y)\widehat{T}^{\nu\mu}(y)
and
\Delta\beta_{\mu}(y)=\beta_{\mu}(y)-\beta_{\mu}(x)
.

In the following, we assume that
\Delta\beta_{\mu}(y)
is small on
\Sigma_{\text{FO}}
, which is justified when
\beta_{\mu}
varies slowly within the correlation lengths between
\widehat{O}
and the operator
\widehat{T}^{\mu\nu}
. Meanwhile, we assume that
\Omega_{\rho\sigma}(y)
is also small on the hypersurface
\Sigma_{\text{FO}}
, which could be of the same order as
\Delta\beta_{\mu}(y)
(this is supported by the fact that the observed spin polarization of hadrons is small). Thus eq. (
35
) can be calculated in a perturbative way in derivative expansion using the power counting rules

\displaystyle\Delta\beta_{\nu}(y)

\displaystyle=(y-x)^{\alpha_{1}}\partial_{\alpha_{1}}\beta_{\nu}(x)+\frac{1}{2}(y-x)^{\alpha_{1}}(y-x)^{\alpha_{2}}\partial_{\alpha_{1}}\partial_{\alpha_{2}}\beta_{\nu}(x)+\cdots=\mathcal{O}(\partial)\,,

(38)

\displaystyle\Omega_{\rho\sigma}(y)

\displaystyle=\Omega_{\rho\sigma}(x)+(y-x)^{\alpha_{1}}\partial_{\alpha_{1}}\Omega_{\rho\sigma}(x)+\cdots=\mathcal{O}(\partial)\,.

(39)

It should be emphasized that in the current content, the gradient expansion is equivalent to the
\hbar
expansion.

Using the cumulant expansion

\displaystyle e^{\widehat{A}+\widehat{B}}

\displaystyle=e^{\widehat{A}}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\widehat{B}_{n}\,,\hskip 20.00003pt\widehat{B}_{0}=1\,,

(40)

\displaystyle\widehat{B}_{n}

\displaystyle=\int^{1}_{0}d\lambda_{1}\int^{\lambda_{1}}_{0}d\lambda_{2}\cdots\int^{\lambda_{n-1}}_{0}d\lambda_{n}\widehat{B}(\lambda_{1})\cdots\widehat{B}(\lambda_{n}),\hskip 10.00002ptn\geq 1

(41)

with
\widehat{B}(\lambda_{i})\equiv e^{-\lambda_{i}\widehat{A}}\widehat{B}e^{\lambda_{i}\widehat{A}}
, we rewrite eq. (
35
) as

O(x)=\frac{\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\left<\widehat{B}_{n}\widehat{O}(x)\right>_{0}}{\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\left<\widehat{B}_{n}\right>_{0}}\,,

(42)

where
\left<\cdots\right>_{0}
denotes the expectation value with density operator
\widehat{\rho}_{0}=e^{\widehat{A}}/Z_{0}
with
Z_{0}=\Tr e^{\widehat{A}}
. In our power counting scheme,
\widehat{A}
is
\mathcal{O}(1)
and
\widehat{B}
contains an
\mathcal{O}(\partial)
term as well as higher order terms
\mathcal{O}(\partial^{n})
with
n=2,3,4,...
, therefore we find that the lowest order part of
\widehat{B}_{n}
is
\mathcal{O}(\partial^{n})
. This allows us to expand the ensemble average
O(x)
in powers of
\partial

O(x)=O^{(0)}(x)+O^{(1)}(x)+O^{(2)}(x)+\mathcal{O}(\partial^{3})\,,

(43)

where we denote

\displaystyle O^{(0)}(x)

\displaystyle=\left<\widehat{O}(x)\right>_{0}\equiv\frac{1}{Z_{0}}\Tr\left(e^{\widehat{A}}\widehat{O}(x)\right)\,,

(44)

\displaystyle O^{(1)}(x)

\displaystyle=\left<\widehat{B}_{1}\widehat{O}(x)\right>_{0,c}\equiv\left<\widehat{B}_{1}\widehat{O}(x)\right>_{0}-\left<\widehat{B}_{1}\right>_{0}O^{(0)}(x)\,,

(45)

\displaystyle O^{(2)}(x)

\displaystyle=\left<\widehat{B}_{2}\widehat{O}(x)\right>_{0,c}\equiv\left<\widehat{B}_{2}\widehat{O}(x)\right>_{0}-\left<\widehat{B}_{2}\right>_{0}O^{(0)}(x)-\left<\widehat{B}_{1}\right>_{0}O^{(1)}(x)\,.

(46)

In the above expressions,
\left<\cdots\right>_{0,c}
represents the connected part of the correlator.
The term “connected” is not entirely coincidental. One may notice that eq. (
42
) resembles Feynman’s path integral formula in perturbative QFT, where terms in the denominator are called vacuum fluctuations and can cancel out the vacuum fluctuations in the numerator.

We note that
\widehat{B}_{n}
contains not only
\mathcal{O}(\partial^{n})
terms but also
\mathcal{O}(\partial^{n^{\prime}})
terms with
n^{\prime}=n+1,n+2,\cdots
. For clarification, we introduce operator
\widehat{\mathcal{B}}_{n}
to represent precisely the
\mathcal{O}(\partial^{n})
terms in
\widehat{B}_{n}
. The relations eqs. (
43
)-(
46
) remain valid for
\widehat{\mathcal{B}}_{n}
. The leading two
\widehat{\mathcal{B}}_{n}
’s are given by

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{B}}_{1}\equiv

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}d\lambda_{1}\int_{\Sigma_{{\rm FO}}}d\Sigma_{\mu_{1}}(y_{1})\left[-\partial_{\alpha_{1}}\beta_{\nu_{1}}(x)(y_{1}-x)^{\alpha_{1}}\widehat{T}^{\mu_{1}\nu_{1}}(y_{1}^{(\beta)})+\frac{1}{2}\Omega_{\rho_{1}\sigma_{1}}(x)\widehat{S}^{\mu_{1}\rho_{1}\sigma_{1}}(y_{1}^{(\beta)})\right],

(47)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\widehat{\mathcal{B}}_{2}\equiv&\int_{0}^{1}d\lambda_{1}\int_{0}^{\lambda_{1}}d\lambda_{2}\int_{\Sigma_{{\rm FO}}}d\Sigma_{\mu_{1}}(y_{1})d\Sigma_{\mu_{2}}(y_{2})\\
&\times\left[\partial_{\alpha_{1}}\beta_{\nu_{1}}(x)\partial_{\alpha_{2}}\beta_{\nu_{2}}(x)(y_{1}-x)^{\alpha_{1}}(y_{2}-x)^{\alpha_{2}}\widehat{T}^{\mu_{1}\nu_{1}}(y_{1}^{(\beta)})\widehat{T}^{\mu_{2}\nu_{2}}(y_{2}^{(\beta)})\right.\\
&-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\alpha_{1}}\beta_{\nu_{1}}(x)\Omega_{\rho_{2}\sigma_{2}}(x)(y_{1}-x)^{\alpha_{1}}\widehat{T}^{\mu_{1}\nu_{1}}(y_{1}^{(\beta)})\widehat{S}^{\mu_{2}\rho_{2}\sigma_{2}}(y_{2}^{(\beta)})\\
&-\frac{1}{2}\Omega_{\rho_{1}\sigma_{1}}(x)\partial_{\alpha_{2}}\beta_{\nu_{2}}(x)(y_{2}-x)^{\alpha_{2}}\widehat{S}^{\mu_{1}\rho_{1}\sigma_{1}}(y_{1}^{(\beta)})\widehat{T}^{\mu_{2}\nu_{2}}(y_{2}^{(\beta)})\\
&\left.+\frac{1}{4}\Omega_{\rho_{1}\sigma_{1}}(x)\Omega_{\rho_{2}\sigma_{2}}(x)\widehat{S}^{\mu_{1}\rho_{1}\sigma_{1}}(y_{1}^{(\beta)})\widehat{S}^{\mu_{2}\rho_{2}\sigma_{2}}(y_{2}^{(\beta)})\right]\\
&+\int_{0}^{1}d\lambda_{1}\int_{\Sigma_{\rm FO}}d\Sigma_{\mu_{1}}(y_{1})\left[-\partial_{\alpha_{1}}\partial_{\alpha_{2}}\beta_{\nu_{1}}(x)\frac{1}{2}(y_{1}-x)^{\alpha_{1}}(y-x)^{\alpha_{2}}\widehat{T}^{\mu_{1}\nu_{1}}(y_{1}^{(\beta)})\right.\\
&+\left.\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\alpha_{1}}\Omega_{\rho_{1}\sigma_{1}}(x)(y_{1}-x)^{\alpha_{1}}\widehat{S}^{\mu_{1}\rho_{1}\sigma_{1}}(y_{1}^{(\beta)})\right]\end{split}

(48)

with
y_{i}^{(\beta)}=y_{i}-i\lambda_{i}\beta(x)
. Such a shift in position is obtained from

e^{\lambda_{i}\beta(x)\cdot\widehat{P}}\widehat{B}(y_{i})e^{-\lambda_{i}\beta(x)\cdot\widehat{P}}=\widehat{B}(y_{i}-i\lambda_{i}\beta(x))\,,

which is because
\widehat{P}_{\mu}
generates space-time translation for operators in the Hilbert space.

4
From MVSD to polarization

In a system described by the LEDO in eq. (
32
), the actual spin density matrix as a function
of the momentum in eq. (
28
) is most conveniently expressed as an integral over
the freeze-out hypersurface (we remind that
\Sigma
is arbitrary) because this is the most convenient
choice to expand the thermodynamic fields. By using the decomposition eq. (
29
)
we thus have

\Theta_{rs}(k)=\frac{\int_{\Sigma_{\rm FO}}d\Sigma\cdot k\,f_{rs}(x,k)}{\int_{\Sigma_{\rm FO}}d\Sigma\cdot k\,f(x,k)}=\frac{\int_{\Sigma_{\rm FO}}d\Sigma\cdot k\,\Theta_{rs}(x,k)f(x,k)}{\int_{\Sigma_{\rm FO}}d\Sigma\cdot k\,f(x,k)}\,.

(49)

By using the definitions eq. (
17
) and eq. (
18
), we then have

\displaystyle S_{\text{rest}}^{i}(k)

\displaystyle=\frac{\int_{\Sigma_{\rm FO}}d\Sigma\cdot k\,S_{\text{rest}}^{i}(x,k)f(x,k)}{\int_{\Sigma_{\rm FO}}d\Sigma\cdot k\,f(x,k)}\,,

(50)

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{T}}_{2,m}(k)

\displaystyle=\frac{\int_{\Sigma_{\rm FO}}d\Sigma\cdot k\,{\mathfrak{T}}_{2,m}(x,k)f(x,k)}{\int_{\Sigma_{\rm FO}}d\Sigma\cdot k\,f(x,k)}\,,

(51)

where the integrands are obtained by tracing the spin density matrix in phase space instead of the
properly defined one, in eq. (
17
) and eq. (
18
).

Replacing the spin density matrix with the spin density matrix in phase space in eq. (
17
) and eq. (
20
)
we obtain

\displaystyle S_{\text{rest}}^{1}(x,k)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{f(x,k)}\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}2\sqrt{2}\imaginary f^{(2i+1)}_{01}(x,k)\,,

(52)

\displaystyle S_{\text{rest}}^{2}(x,k)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{f(x,k)}\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}2f^{(2i+1)}_{11}(x,k)\,,

(53)

\displaystyle S_{\text{rest}}^{3}(x,k)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{f(x,k)}\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}2\sqrt{2}\real f^{(2i+1)}_{01}(x,k)\,,

(54)

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{T}}_{2,0}(x,k)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{f(x,k)}\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}\sqrt{\frac{2}{3}}\left[f^{(2i+2)}_{11}(x,k)-f^{(2i+2)}_{00}(x,k)\right]\,,

(55)

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{T}}_{2,1}(x,k)=-{\mathfrak{T}}^{*}_{2,-1}(x,k)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{f(x,k)}\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}(-\sqrt{2})f^{(2i+2)}_{01}(x,k)\,,

(56)

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{T}}_{2,2}(x,k)={\mathfrak{T}}^{*}_{2,-2}(x,k)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{f(x,k)}\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}f^{(2i+2)}_{-11}(x,k)\,,

(57)

where
f(x,k)=\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}[2f_{11}^{(2i)}(x,k)+f_{00}^{(2i)}(x,k)]
because
f_{-1-1}^{(2i)}(x,k)=f_{11}^{(2i)}(x,k)
and

f_{rr}^{(2i+1)}(x,k)=-f_{-r-r}^{(2i+1)}(x,k)
(see eq. (
58
)). Here
f_{rs}^{(i)}
denotes the
\mathcal{O}(\partial^{i})
part of the whole MVSD.
The MVSD at zeroth order in gradients should be
f_{rs}^{(0)}=f_{00}^{(0)}\delta_{rs}
. Note that, in eqs. (
52
)-(
57
), the even (odd) order terms do not contribute to the numerators of vector (tensor) polarization. This is due to the following space-time reversal property of MVSD (For details of the derivation, see appendix
)

f^{(n)}_{r,s}(x,k)=(-1)^{r+s+n}f^{(n)}_{-s,-r}(x,k)\,.

(58)

The leading order contributions to the vector and tensor polarization are

\displaystyle\left\{S_{\text{rest}}^{1},S_{\text{rest}}^{2},S_{\text{rest}}^{3}\right\}

\displaystyle\approx\frac{1}{f^{(0)}}\left\{2\sqrt{2}\imaginary f^{(1)}_{01},\,2f^{(1)}_{11},\,2\sqrt{2}\real f^{(1)}_{01}\right\}\,,

(59)

\displaystyle\left\{{\mathfrak{T}}_{2,0},{\mathfrak{T}}_{2,1},{\mathfrak{T}}_{2,2}\right\}

\displaystyle\approx\frac{1}{f^{(0)}}\left\{\sqrt{\frac{2}{3}}(f^{(2)}_{11}-f^{(2)}_{00}),\,-\sqrt{2}f^{(2)}_{01},\,f^{(2)}_{-11}\right\}\,,

(60)

where
f^{(0)}
is the zeroth order scalar distribution
f^{(0)}=\sum_{r=0,\pm 1}f_{rr}^{(0)}=3f_{00}^{(0)}
.
Accordingly, the spin alignment in phase space is given by

\Theta_{00}(x,k)-\frac{1}{3}\approx\frac{2(f^{(2)}_{00}-f^{(2)}_{11})}{3f^{(0)}}.

(61)

Therefore, the evaluation of eq. (
61
) does require the expansion of the MSVD at the second order.

5
Zeroth- and first-order results

In this section, we focus on the zeroth and first-order parts of the MVSD. The zeroth order part can be easily calculated using
\left<\widehat{a}^{r\dagger}_{\bm{k}}\widehat{a}^{s}_{\bm{q}}\right>_{0}=(2E_{\bm{k}})(2\pi)^{3}\delta^{(3)}({\bm{k}}-{\bm{q}})\delta^{rs}n_{B}(\beta(x)\cdot k)
. The result reads

{f}_{rs}^{(0)}(x,k)=\delta(k^{2}-m^{2})\theta(k^{0})\delta_{rs}n_{B}(\beta(x)\cdot k)\,,

(62)

where
n_{B}(\beta(x)\cdot k)
is the Bose-Einstein distribution, i.e.,
1/(e^{\beta(x)\cdot k}-1)
.

For the MVSD at first order in gradients, we split it into two parts as
f^{(1)}_{rs}=f^{(1)}_{rs}|_{T}+f^{(1)}_{rs}|_{S}
with

\displaystyle f^{(1)}_{rs}|_{T}

\displaystyle\equiv-\int_{0}^{1}d\lambda_{1}\int_{\Sigma_{{\rm FO}}}d\Sigma_{\mu_{1}}(y_{1})[\partial_{\alpha_{1}}\beta_{\nu_{1}}(x)](y_{1}-x)^{\alpha_{1}}\left<\widehat{T}^{\mu_{1}\nu_{1}}(y_{1}^{(\beta)})\widehat{f}_{rs}(x,k)\right>_{0,c}\,,\hskip 9.24994pt

(63)

\displaystyle f^{(1)}_{rs}|_{S}

\displaystyle\equiv\int_{0}^{1}d\lambda_{1}\int_{\Sigma_{{\rm FO}}}d\Sigma_{\mu_{1}}(y_{1})\Omega_{\rho_{1}\sigma_{1}}(x)\left<\frac{1}{2}\widehat{S}^{\mu_{1}\rho_{1}\sigma_{1}}(y_{1}^{(\beta)})\widehat{f}_{rs}(x,k)\right>_{0,c}\,,

(64)

where
f^{(1)}_{rs}|_{T}
(
f^{(1)}_{rs}|_{S}
) represents the contribution from the correlation between the energy-momentum operator
(the spin operator) and the MVSD operator. By substituting eqs. (
24
), (
25
), (
30
), and the quantized field operator eq. (
4
) into
f^{(1)}_{rs}|_{T}
, we obtain

\begin{split}f^{(1)}_{rs}|_{T}=&\epsilon^{\gamma_{3}*}_{r}(k)\epsilon^{\gamma_{0}}_{s}(k)\int_{0}^{1}d\lambda_{1}\int_{\Sigma_{{\rm FO}}}d\Sigma_{\mu_{1}}(y_{1})\left[\partial_{\alpha_{1}}\beta_{\nu_{1}}(x)\right](y_{1}-x)^{\alpha_{1}}\\
&\times\prod_{i=0}^{3}\left(\sum_{a_{i}=-1}^{1}\int\frac{d^{\,3}{{\bm{p}}_{i}}}{(2\pi)^{3}}\frac{1}{2E_{{\bm{p}}_{i}}}\right)(2\pi)^{3}\delta^{(4)}\left(k-\frac{p_{1}}{2}-\frac{p_{2}}{2}\right)\Big\{\Big[p_{1}^{\mu_{1}}p_{2}^{\nu_{1}}\eta^{\gamma_{1}\gamma_{2}}\\
&-p_{1}^{\gamma_{2}}p_{2}^{\nu_{1}}\eta^{\mu_{1}\rho_{1}}-\frac{1}{2}\left(p_{1}\cdot p_{2}\right)\eta^{\mu_{1}\nu_{1}}\eta^{\gamma_{1}\gamma_{2}}+\frac{1}{2}\eta^{\mu_{1}\nu_{1}}\left(p_{1}^{\gamma_{2}}p_{2}^{\gamma_{1}}+m^{2}\eta^{\gamma_{1}\gamma_{2}}\right)\Big]e^{i(p_{0}-p_{3})\cdot x}\\
&\times\Big[\left<\widehat{a}_{{\bm{p}}_{1}}^{a_{1}}\widehat{a}^{a_{0}\dagger}_{{\bm{p}}_{0}}\right>_{0}\left<\widehat{a}_{{\bm{p}}_{2}}^{a_{2}\dagger}\widehat{a}^{a_{3}}_{{\bm{p}}_{3}}\right>_{0}\epsilon_{\gamma_{0}}^{a_{0}*}(p_{0})\epsilon_{\gamma_{1}}^{a_{1}}(p_{1})\epsilon_{\gamma_{2}}^{a_{2}*}(p_{2})\epsilon_{\gamma_{3}}^{a_{3}}(p_{3})e^{-i(p_{1}-p_{2})\cdot(y_{1}-i\lambda_{1}\beta(x))}\\
&+\left<\widehat{a}_{{\bm{p}}_{1}}^{a_{1}\dagger}\widehat{a}^{a_{3}}_{{\bm{p}}_{3}}\right>_{0}\left<\widehat{a}_{{\bm{p}}_{2}}^{a_{2}}\widehat{a}^{a_{0}\dagger}_{{\bm{p}}_{0}}\right>_{0}\epsilon_{\gamma_{0}}^{a_{0}*}(p_{0})\epsilon_{\gamma_{1}}^{a_{1}*}(p_{1})\epsilon_{\gamma_{2}}^{a_{2}}(p_{2})\epsilon_{\gamma_{3}}^{a_{3}}(p_{3})e^{i(p_{1}-p_{2})\cdot(y_{1}-i\lambda_{1}\beta(x))}\Big]\Big\}.\end{split}

(65)

For the integration over
\Sigma_{{\rm FO}}
in the Cooper-Frye type formulas, we consider a scenario that
\Xi
, the space-like part of
\Sigma_{{\rm FO}}
(see figure
1
),
is a flat hyperplane and is sufficiently large, while the contribution from
\Sigma_{t}
, the time-like part of
\Sigma_{{\rm FO}}
, can be neglected.
If these conditions are relaxed,
\Xi
’s non-planarity and the evaluation of
f_{rs}(x,k)
with
x\in\Sigma_{t}

will bring additional complexity to our calculation (see appendix
), which will be studied in a separate paper.
Based on the above assumptions, for the integration over
\Sigma_{{\rm FO}}
in an expression like eq. (
65
), we can replace
\Sigma_{\rm FO}
by hyperplane
\Xi
,
which can be verified in appendix
.
Without loss of generality, in the following, we take the hyperplane
\Xi
to be perpendicular to the time direction

2
2

2

Due to the Lorentz covariance of the Wigner function, the coordinate time direction,
{\hat{t}}^{\mu}
, in our expressions
can always be replaced by the normal vector for a general flat hyperplane.
,
{\hat{t}}^{\mu}=(1,\bm{0})
, namely the isochronous freeze-out

3
3

3

If the integration over the four-volume
\Omega
in figure
1
does not contribute to spin polarization
Becattini:2021suc
,
we can replace the integration over
\Sigma_{\rm FO}
by the integration over
\Xi_{B}
.
Our results can be applied to this scenario as well by simply replacing
\Xi
by
\Xi_{B}
.
.
This leads to

\begin{split}&\int_{\Xi}d\Xi_{\mu_{1}}(y_{1})\prod_{i=1}^{2}\left(\int\frac{d^{\,3}{{\bm{p}}_{i}}}{(2\pi)^{3}}\right)(y_{1}-x)^{\alpha_{1}}e^{-i(p_{1}-p_{2})\cdot(y_{1}-x)}\\
=&{\hat{t}}_{\mu_{1}}\prod_{i=1}^{2}\left(\int\frac{d^{\,3}{{\bm{p}}_{i}}}{(2\pi)^{3}}\right)(2\pi)^{3}\delta^{(3)}\left({\bm{p}}_{1}-{\bm{p}}_{2}\right)\left(-\frac{i}{2}\right)\left({\nabla}_{1}^{\alpha_{1}}-{\nabla}_{2}^{\alpha_{1}}\right),\end{split}

(66)

where the transverse derivative operator is defined as
{\nabla}_{i}^{\alpha_{j}}\equiv\partial_{i}^{\alpha_{j}}-\hat{t}^{\alpha_{j}}(\hat{t}\cdot\partial_{i})
with
\partial_{i}^{\alpha_{j}}=\partial/\partial p_{i,\alpha_{j}}
being the covariant momentum derivative vector. Noting that
{\nabla}_{i}^{0}
is always zero because the four-momentum
p_{i}^{\mu}
is restricted on the normal mass-shell with
p_{i}^{0}=\sqrt{{\bm{p}}_{i}^{2}+m^{2}}
, indicating that
p_{i}^{0}
is not an independent parameter. Therefore, for
\alpha_{1}=0
, both sides of the equation are identically zero.
When calculating eq. (
65
), we first do the integration over
{\bm{p}}_{0}
and
{\bm{p}}_{3}
, then an integral by part shown in eq. (
66
). We note that the momentum derivatives in eq. (
66
) also act on
\delta^{(4)}\left(k-\frac{p_{1}}{2}-\frac{p_{2}}{2}\right)
. However, one can check that the delta function commutes with the partial derivatives, which allows us to move it outside of the momentum derivatives.
Using
\left<\widehat{a}^{r\dagger}_{\bm{k}}\widehat{a}^{s}_{\bm{q}}\right>_{0}=(2E_{\bm{k}})(2\pi)^{3}\delta^{(3)}({\bm{k}}-{\bm{q}})\delta^{rs}n_{B}
and
\left<\widehat{a}^{r}_{\bm{k}}\widehat{a}^{s\dagger}_{\bm{q}}\right>_{0}=(2E_{\bm{k}})(2\pi)^{3}\delta^{(3)}({\bm{k}}-{\bm{q}})\delta^{rs}(1+n_{B})
, eq. (
65
) is simplified to the following form

f^{(1)}_{rs}|_{T}(x,k)=\frac{i}{2}\delta(k^{2}-m^{2})\theta(k^{0})n_{B}(1+n_{B})\epsilon^{\gamma_{3}*}_{r}(k)\epsilon^{\gamma_{0}}_{s}(k)\left\{{\hat{t}}_{[\gamma_{0}}[\partial_{\gamma_{3}]}\beta_{\nu_{1}}(x)]\hat{k}^{\nu_{1}}\right\}

(67)

with
n_{B}
standing for
n_{B}(\beta(x)\cdot k)
.
(The detailed derivation of eq. (
67
) is tedious but straightforward. We first do the integration over
{\bm{p}}_{0},{\bm{p}}_{1},{\bm{p}}_{2},{\bm{p}}_{3}
, then the integration over
\lambda_{1}
.)
The calculation of
f_{rs}^{(1)}|_{S}
is completely the same, which gives

f^{(1)}_{rs}|_{S}(x,k)=\frac{i}{2}\delta(k^{2}-m^{2})\theta(k^{0})n_{B}(1+n_{B})\epsilon^{\gamma_{3}*}_{r}(k)\epsilon^{\gamma_{0}}_{s}(k)\left\{2\Omega_{\gamma_{0}\gamma_{3}}+\hat{k}^{\rho_{1}}\Omega_{\rho_{1}[\gamma_{0}}{\hat{t}}_{\gamma 3]}\right\}\,.

(68)

As the terms in the curly braces of eqs. (
67
)-(
68
) are both antisymmetric w.r.t.
\gamma_{0}\leftrightarrow\gamma_{3}
, we have

f_{rs}^{(1)}(x,k)=-f_{-s,-r}^{(1)}(x,k)\,,

which agrees with eq. (
58
).
Such a relation implies that
f_{00}^{(1)}(x,k)=0
and
f_{11}^{(1)}(x,k)+f_{-1,-1}^{(1)}(x,k)=0
, leading to a vanishing spin alignment at the first order in gradients, which agrees with the results in refs.
Yang:2017sdk
;
Sheng:2019kmk
.

6
Diagrammatic scheme

In order to derive a formula for the spin alignment, we need to evaluate the second-order MVSD since the zeroth- and first-order results do not yield a tensor polarization (see section
4
).
For this purpose, we develop a diagrammatic scheme analogous to the Feynman diagram to calculate
f_{rs}(x,k)
order by order in space-time gradients.

Similar to eqs. (
63
)-(
64
), we split the second-order MVSD into six terms, respectively related to the six terms of
\widehat{\mathcal{B}}_{2}
given in eq. (
48
),

f^{(2)}_{rs}\equiv\left<\widehat{\mathcal{B}}_{2}\widehat{f}_{rs}\right>_{0,c}=f^{(2)}_{rs}|_{TT}+f^{(2)}_{rs}|_{TS}+f^{(2)}_{rs}|_{ST}+f^{(2)}_{rs}|_{SS}+f^{(2)}_{rs}|_{T}+f^{(2)}_{rs}|_{S}\,,

(69)

where “
T
” and “
S
” represent the energy-momentum and spin operators, respectively.
The term
f^{(2)}_{rs}|_{TT}
, for example, denotes the contribution from the correlation between two energy-momentum operators and the MVSD operator.
Another term,
f^{(2)}_{rs}|_{TS}
, is the contribution from the correlation between one energy-momentum operator, one spin operator, and the MVSD operator.
The other terms can be understood similarly. Therefore, we would expect (and we will show) that
f^{(2)}_{rs}|_{TT}\propto(\partial\beta)(\partial\beta)
,

f^{(2)}_{rs}|_{TS}\propto(\partial\beta)\Omega
,
f^{(2)}_{rs}|_{ST}\propto\Omega(\partial\beta)
,
f^{(2)}_{rs}|_{SS}\propto\Omega\Omega
,

f^{(2)}_{rs}|_{T}\propto\partial\partial\beta
, and
f^{(2)}_{rs}|_{T}\propto\partial\Omega
.

Let us take
f^{(2)}_{rs}|_{TT}
as an example. It contains a term as follows

\begin{split}&\,\epsilon^{\gamma_{5}*}_{r}(k)\epsilon^{\gamma_{0}}_{s}(k)\left[\partial_{\alpha_{1}}\beta_{\nu_{1}}(x)\right]\left[\partial_{\alpha_{2}}\beta_{\nu_{2}}(x)\right]\int_{0}^{1}d\lambda_{1}\int_{0}^{\lambda_{1}}d\lambda_{2}\prod_{i=0}^{5}\left(\sum_{a_{i}=-1}^{+1}\int\frac{d^{\,3}{{\bm{p}}_{i}}}{(2\pi)^{3}}\right)\\
&\times\int_{\Xi}d\Xi_{\mu_{1}}(y_{1})d\Xi_{\mu_{2}}(y_{2})(y_{1}-x)^{\alpha_{1}}(y_{2}-x)^{\alpha_{2}}e^{i\,(p_{0}-p_{5})\cdot x}e^{-i(p_{1}+p_{2})\cdot y_{1}}\\
&\times e^{+i(p_{3}+p_{4})\cdot y_{2}}(2\pi)^{3}\delta^{(4)}\left(k-\frac{p_{0}}{2}-\frac{p_{5}}{2}\right)t\,^{\mu_{1}\nu_{1}}_{(1)~\gamma_{1}\gamma_{2}}(p_{1},p_{2})t\,^{\mu_{2}\nu_{2}}_{(2)~\gamma_{3}\gamma_{4}}(-p_{3},-p_{4})\\
&\times\epsilon_{\gamma_{0}}^{a_{0}*}(p_{0})\epsilon^{\gamma_{1}}_{a_{1}}(p_{1})\epsilon^{\gamma_{2}}_{a_{2}}(p_{2})\epsilon^{\gamma_{3}*}_{a_{3}}(p_{3})\epsilon^{\gamma_{4}*}_{a_{4}}(p_{4})\epsilon_{\gamma_{5}}^{a_{5}}(p_{5})\left<\widehat{a}^{a_{1}}_{{\bm{p}}_{1}}\widehat{a}^{a_{2}}_{{\bm{p}}_{2}}\widehat{a}^{a_{3}\dagger}_{{\bm{p}}_{3}}\widehat{a}^{a_{4}\dagger}_{{\bm{p}}_{4}}\widehat{a}^{a_{0}\dagger}_{{\bm{p}}_{0}}\widehat{a}^{a_{5}}_{{\bm{p}}_{5}}\right>_{0,c}\,,\end{split}

(70)

where we denote

\begin{split}t\,^{\mu_{1}\nu_{1}}_{(1)~\gamma_{1}\gamma_{2}}(p_{1},p_{2})=&\frac{e^{-\lambda_{1}(p_{1}+p_{2})\cdot\beta}}{2E_{{\bm{p}}_{1}}2E_{{\bm{p}}_{2}}}\Big[p_{1}^{\mu_{1}}p_{2}^{\nu_{1}}\eta_{\gamma_{1}\gamma_{2}}-{p_{1}}_{\gamma_{2}}p_{2}^{\nu_{1}}\eta^{\mu_{1}}_{~~\gamma_{1}}\\
&-\frac{1}{2}(p_{1}\cdot p_{2})\eta_{\gamma_{1}\gamma_{2}}\eta^{\mu_{1}\nu_{1}}+\frac{1}{2}{p_{1}}_{\gamma_{2}}{p_{2}}_{\gamma_{1}}\eta^{\mu_{1}\nu_{1}}-\frac{1}{2}m^{2}\eta_{\gamma_{1}\gamma_{2}}\eta^{\mu_{1}\nu_{1}}\Big]\,,\end{split}

(71)

which comes from
\widehat{T}^{\mu_{1}\nu_{1}}(x)
;
t\,^{\mu_{2}\nu_{2}}_{(2)~\gamma_{3}\gamma_{4}}(p_{3},p_{4})
is completely the same but from
\widehat{T}^{\mu_{2}\nu_{2}}(x)
.
We emphasize that the expression (
70
) is just one part of the whole
f^{(2)}_{rs}|_{TT}
.
The full result for
f^{(2)}_{rs}|_{TT}
will be shown in section
7
with a diagrammatic scheme.

Utilizing the Wick theorem, we obtain

\begin{split}&\left<\widehat{a}^{a_{1}}_{{\bm{p}}_{1}}\widehat{a}^{a_{2}}_{{\bm{p}}_{2}}\widehat{a}^{a_{3}\dagger}_{{\bm{p}}_{3}}\widehat{a}^{a_{4}\dagger}_{{\bm{p}}_{4}}\widehat{a}^{a_{0}\dagger}_{{\bm{p}}_{0}}\widehat{a}^{a_{5}}_{{\bm{p}}_{5}}\right>_{0,c}\\
=&\left<\widehat{a}^{a_{1}}_{{\bm{p}}_{1}}\widehat{a}^{a_{0}\dagger}_{{\bm{p}}_{0}}\right>_{0}\left<\widehat{a}^{a_{2}}_{{\bm{p}}_{2}}\widehat{a}^{a_{3}\dagger}_{{\bm{p}}_{3}}\right>_{0}\left<\widehat{a}^{a_{4}\dagger}_{{\bm{p}}_{4}}\widehat{a}^{a_{5}}_{{\bm{p}}_{5}}\right>_{0}+\left<\widehat{a}^{a_{1}}_{{\bm{p}}_{1}}\widehat{a}^{a_{4}\dagger}_{{\bm{p}}_{4}}\right>_{0}\left<\widehat{a}^{a_{2}}_{{\bm{p}}_{2}}\widehat{a}^{a_{0}\dagger}_{{\bm{p}}_{0}}\right>_{0}\left<\widehat{a}^{a_{3}\dagger}_{{\bm{p}}_{3}}\widehat{a}^{a_{5}}_{{\bm{p}}_{5}}\right>_{0}\\
&+\left<\widehat{a}^{a_{1}}_{{\bm{p}}_{1}}\widehat{a}^{a_{3}\dagger}_{{\bm{p}}_{3}}\right>_{0}\left<\widehat{a}^{a_{2}}_{{\bm{p}}_{2}}\widehat{a}^{a_{0}\dagger}_{{\bm{p}}_{0}}\right>_{0}\left<\widehat{a}^{a_{4}\dagger}_{{\bm{p}}_{4}}\widehat{a}^{a_{5}}_{{\bm{p}}_{5}}\right>_{0}+\left<\widehat{a}^{a_{1}}_{{\bm{p}}_{1}}\widehat{a}^{a_{0}\dagger}_{{\bm{p}}_{0}}\right>_{0}\left<\widehat{a}^{a_{2}}_{{\bm{p}}_{2}}\widehat{a}^{a_{4}\dagger}_{{\bm{p}}_{4}}\right>_{0}\left<\widehat{a}^{a_{3}\dagger}_{{\bm{p}}_{3}}\widehat{a}^{a_{5}}_{{\bm{p}}_{5}}\right>_{0}\,.\end{split}

(72)

We can use diagrams to express these different terms. For instance, the first term is represented by

\left<\widehat{a}^{a_{1}}_{{\bm{p}}_{1}}\widehat{a}^{a_{0}\dagger}_{{\bm{p}}_{0}}\right>_{0}\left<\widehat{a}^{a_{2}}_{{\bm{p}}_{2}}\widehat{a}^{a_{3}\dagger}_{{\bm{p}}_{3}}\right>_{0}\left<\widehat{a}^{a_{4}\dagger}_{{\bm{p}}_{4}}\widehat{a}^{a_{5}}_{{\bm{p}}_{5}}\right>_{0}=\hbox to124.02pt{\vbox to68.62pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 18.257pt\lower-26.28539pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1}

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}},

(73)

where the left arrow “
a_{1},\,p_{1}\leftarrow a_{2},\,p_{2}
” represents
\left<\widehat{a}^{a_{1}}_{{\bm{p}}_{1}}\widehat{a}^{a_{2}\dagger}_{{\bm{p}}_{2}}\right>_{0}

and the right arrow “
a_{1},\,{\bm{p}}_{1}\rightarrow a_{2},\,{\bm{p}}_{2}
” represents
\left<\widehat{a}^{a_{1}\dagger}_{p_{1}}\widehat{a}^{a_{2}}_{p_{2}}\right>_{0}
.
Since a mean value like
\left<\widehat{a}^{a_{1}}_{{\bm{p}}_{1}}\widehat{a}^{a_{2}\dagger}_{{\bm{p}}_{2}}\right>_{0}
is proportional to
\delta_{a_{1}a_{2}}\delta^{(3)}({\bm{p}}_{1}-{\bm{p}}_{2})
,
the momenta and the spin indices connected by a line should be equal, respectively.
On the other hand, the diagram element

p_{1}
p_{2}
a_{1}
a_{2}

(74)

represents any c-number function that multiplying with
\widehat{a}^{a_{1}}_{{\bm{p}}_{1}}\widehat{a}^{a_{2}}_{{\bm{p}}_{2}}
.
The arrow going into the box represents the annihilator
\widehat{a}
, while the arrow going out of the box represents creator
\widehat{a}^{\dagger}
.

Furthermore, we can generalize the rules of the diagram to incorporate other terms in the expression (
70
).
We introduce some details to the diagram, namely

(75)

We clarify the meaning of various symbols in the following. By comparing with eq. (
70
), we can infer that each line entering (exiting) the boxes should carry the polarization vector (the conjugate of polarization vector), namely

\hbox to34.17pt{\vbox to50.08pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 17.21434pt\lower-7.71318pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1}
{{}}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{{}{}}{}}}{{

(76)

Since we will sum over the spin indices, each line yields a term of
(-\eta^{\gamma_{i}\gamma_{j}}+p_{i}^{\gamma_{i}}p_{j}^{\gamma_{j}}/m^{2})
.

Each operator “
T
” in the red box produces a
t\,^{\mu_{l}\nu_{l}}_{(l)~\gamma_{i}\gamma_{j}}(p_{i},p_{j})

\lxSVG@closescope {

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{{}{}}{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{{}{}}{}}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{{}{}}{}}{{{}}{{}}}{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{{}{}}{}}{{{}}{{}}}{

{}{}}{}{}{{}{}}}}}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.8pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 -11.72 C 0 -27.07 0 -35.73 0 -49.7}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1}
{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{{}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{0.0}{-1.0}{1.0}{0.0}{0.0pt}{-26.43047pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(0.0 -1.0 1.0 0.0 0 -36.57)} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray=none,stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin=round} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -7.66 0 L -1.79 2.18 L -3.45 0 L -1.79 -2.18 Z}{} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}
\lxSVG@closescope }\lxSVG@closescope }{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\sim\,t\,^{\mu_{l}\nu_{l}}_{(l)~\gamma_{i}\gamma_{j}}(p_{i},p_{j})

(77)

with
(l)
standing for the serial number of the red box.
If
p_{i}
enters the box and
p_{j}
exits the box, this should yield
t~^{\mu_{l}\nu_{l}}_{(l)~\gamma_{i}\gamma_{j}}(p_{i},-p_{j})
, which resembles the vortex rule in the standard Feynman rules.

Based on the above rules, we have included most of the terms in eq. (
70
), except for the hydrodynamic parameters,
\partial\beta
, the integrals over “
\lambda
”s, and the integrals over space and momenta.
Of these, the last is the most complex. Due to space limitations, we present three examples

p_{i}
p_{j}
\begin{aligned} &\int_{\Xi}d\Xi_{\mu_{l}}(y_{l})\int\frac{d^{\,3}{{\bm{p}}_{i}}}{(2\pi)^{3}}\frac{d^{\,3}{{\bm{p}}_{j}}}{(2\pi)^{3}}e^{-i\,(p_{i}+p_{j})\cdot(y_{l}-x)}\\
=&\int\frac{d^{\,3}{{\bm{p}}_{i}}}{(2\pi)^{3}}\frac{d^{\,3}{{\bm{p}}_{j}}}{(2\pi)^{3}}(2\pi)^{3}\delta^{(3)}({\bm{p}}_{i}+{\bm{p}}_{j}){\hat{t}}_{\mu_{l}}\,,\end{aligned}

(78)

D_{(l)}^{\alpha_{m}}
p_{i}
p_{j}
\begin{aligned} &\int_{\Xi}d\Xi_{\mu_{l}}(y_{l})(y_{l}-x)^{\alpha_{m}}e^{-i\,(p_{i}+p_{j})\cdot(y_{l}-x)}\\
\sim~&(2\pi)^{3}\delta^{(3)}({\bm{p}}_{i}+{\bm{p}}_{j}){\hat{t}}_{\mu_{l}}\left(-\frac{i}{2}\right)\left({\nabla}_{i}^{\alpha_{m}}+{\nabla}_{j}^{\alpha_{m}}\right)\,,\end{aligned}

(79)

D_{(l)}^{\alpha_{m}}
p_{i}
p_{j}
\begin{aligned} &\int_{\Xi}d\Xi_{\mu_{l}}(y_{l})(y_{l}-x)^{\alpha_{m}}e^{-i\,(p_{i}-p_{j})\cdot(y_{l}-x)}\\
\sim~&(2\pi)^{3}\delta^{(3)}({\bm{p}}_{i}-{\bm{p}}_{j}){\hat{t}}_{\mu_{l}}\left(-\frac{i}{2}\right)\left({\nabla}_{i}^{\alpha_{m}}-{\nabla}_{j}^{\alpha_{m}}\right)\end{aligned}

(80)

with “
\sim
” denoting that the calculation is done with the integrals over the momenta,
N
the total number of the red boxes in the diagram,

i
taking a value from
1
to
2N-1
(
j=i+1
) and
m
in the
\alpha_{m}
standing for the serial number of the “
D
”.
To verify these equations, we recommend transforming the variables by
l_{1}=p_{i}-p_{j},\,l_{2}=(p_{i}+p_{j})/2
,
otherwise swapping the delta function and the partial derivatives may be difficult.
Another technique used here is that we multiply an extra exponential term

e^{-i(p_{0}\pm p_{1}\pm\cdots-p_{(2N+1)})\cdot x}\,,

which always equals
1
by the restriction from the delta functions on the lines.
For example, in eq. (
70
), the extra term is
e^{-i(p_{0}-p_{1}-p_{2}+p_{3}+p_{4}-p_{5})\cdot x}
.
With this term, the
x
dependence, except for the hydrodynamic fields, e.g.,
\partial_{\alpha}\beta_{\nu}(x)
, always appears in
(y_{l}-x)
.
Therefore, we can deal with
x
together with
y_{l}
.

Based on those examples, a delta function emerges from each red box,
which constrains the momenta across the red box to be the same (opposite) if the line preserves (reverses) its direction across the box.
Therefore, we denote

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{{}{}}{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{{}{}}{}}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{{}{}}{}}{{{}}{{}}}{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{{}{}}{}}{{{}}{{}}}{

{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{{}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{0.0}{-1.0}{1.0}{0.0}{0.0pt}{-26.43047pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(0.0 -1.0 1.0 0.0 0 -36.57)} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray=none,stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin=round} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -7.66 0 L -1.79 2.18 L -3.45 0 L -1.79 -2.18 Z}{} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}
\lxSVG@closescope }\lxSVG@closescope }{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}D_{(l)}^{\alpha_{m}}=\left(-\frac{i}{2}\right)\left({\nabla}_{i}^{\alpha_{m}}+{\nabla}_{j}^{\alpha_{m}}\right)\,.

These “
D
”s (whether from the same box or not) commute with each other and commute with the delta functions from other boxes.
Therefore, we can apply this rule to diagrams with multiple boxes and multiple “
D
”s, without worrying about the sequence of “
D
”s.
We should note that here
{\nabla}_{i}^{\alpha_{m}}
denotes the transverse momentum derivative with respect to
p_{i}
,
with the time component
{\nabla}_{i}^{0}=0
by its definition.

However, we note that “
D
”s will act on the delta functions from the lines and the
\delta^{(4)}(k-p_{0}/2-p_{(2N+1)}/2)
from the MVSD.
Therefore, the calculation is still not trivial. We need to swap “
D
”s with these delta functions. (For details, see appendix
.)
These swaps convert “
D
”s into

D^{\alpha_{m}}_{(l)}=\left(-\frac{i}{2}\right)\sum_{i=0}^{2N+1}\sigma^{(l)}_{i}{\nabla}^{\alpha_{m}}_{i}\,,

(81)

where
\sum_{i}
denotes summing over all the “
p
”s (in our case, from
p_{0}
to
p_{5}
)
and
\sigma^{(l)}_{i}=\pm 1
(
\sigma_{i}^{(l)}=1
if the line of
p_{i}
points to the box
l
, otherwise
\sigma_{i}^{(l)}=-1
).
We should point out that we have neglected the derivatives acting on
\delta(k^{0}-E_{{\bm{p}}_{0}}/2-E_{{\bm{p}}_{(2N+1)}}/2)
,
indicating that we have neglected the off-shell terms.
After moving all the delta functions, from boxes and lines, to the left-hand side of the derivatives,
we can integrate them out (except for
\delta(k^{0}-E_{{\bm{p}}_{0}}/2-E_{{\bm{p}}_{(2N+1)}}/2)
).
This restricts
p_{i}
to either
k
or
\overline{k}
, where
k^{\mu}=(E_{\bm{k}},{\bm{k}})
and
\overline{k}^{\mu}=(E_{\bm{k}},-{\bm{k}})
. Due to the delta functions, We need to assign
p_{i}=k
if the line of
p_{i}
points to the bottom of the green box, and assign
p_{i}=\overline{k}
otherwise.

In conclusion, the Feynman rules are as follows.

1)
Write out
\widehat{\mathcal{B}}_{n}
and draw all the diagrams by utilizing the Wick Theorem.

2)
MVSD
f
\hskip 9.24994pt\sim\hskip 9.24994pt\delta(k^{2}-m^{2})\theta(k^{0})\frac{2E_{\bm{k}}}{2E_{{\bm{p}}_{1}}2E_{{\bm{p}}_{(2N)}}}\epsilon^{\gamma_{0}}_{s}(k)\epsilon^{\gamma_{(2N+1)}*}_{r}(k)
3)
Lines
\hskip 9.24994pt\sim\hskip 9.24994pt2({\hat{t}}\cdot p_{i})n_{B}(\beta\cdot p_{i})(-\eta^{\gamma_{i}\gamma_{j}}+p_{i}^{\gamma_{i}}p_{i}^{\gamma_{j}}/m^{2})
p_{j}
\gamma_{j}
\gamma_{i}
p_{i}
\hskip 9.24994pt\sim\hskip 9.24994pt2({\hat{t}}\cdot p_{i})\left[n_{B}(\beta\cdot p_{i})+1\right](-\eta^{\gamma_{i}\gamma_{j}}+p_{i}^{\gamma_{i}}p_{i}^{\gamma_{j}}/m^{2})
p_{j}
\gamma_{j}
p_{i}
\gamma_{i}
The momenta connected by the same line should be equal,
p_{i}=p_{j}
.

4)
Vertices
T^{\mu_{l}\nu_{l}}_{(l)}
p_{i}
\gamma_{i}
p_{j}
\gamma_{j}
\begin{aligned} \hskip 9.24994pt\sim\hskip 9.24994ptt\,^{\mu_{l}\nu_{l}}_{(l)~~\gamma_{i}\gamma_{j}}&(p_{i},p_{j})=\frac{e^{-\lambda_{l}\beta\cdot(p_{i}+p_{j})}}{2E_{{\bm{p}}_{i}}2E_{{\bm{p}}_{j}}}\Big[p_{i}^{\mu_{l}}p_{j}^{\nu_{l}}\eta_{\gamma_{i}\gamma_{j}}-p_{i,\gamma_{j}}p_{j}^{\nu_{l}}\eta^{\mu_{l}}_{~~\gamma_{i}}\\
&-\frac{1}{2}(p_{i}\cdot p_{j})\eta^{\mu_{l}\nu_{l}}\eta_{\gamma_{i}\gamma_{j}}+\frac{1}{2}p_{i,\gamma_{j}}p_{j,\gamma_{i}}\eta^{\mu_{l}\nu_{l}}-\frac{1}{2}m^{2}\eta^{\mu_{l}\nu_{l}}\eta_{\gamma_{i}\gamma_{j}}\Big]\end{aligned}
S^{\mu_{l}\rho_{l}\sigma_{l}}_{(l)}
p_{i}
\gamma_{i}
p_{j}
\gamma_{j}
\begin{aligned} \hskip 9.24994pt\sim\hskip 9.24994pts\,^{\mu_{l}\rho_{l}\sigma_{l}}_{(l)~~~~\gamma_{i}\gamma_{j}}&(p_{i},p_{j})=2i\,\frac{e^{-\lambda_{l}\beta\cdot(p_{i}+p_{j})}}{2E_{{\bm{p}}_{i}}2E_{{\bm{p}}_{j}}}\Big(p_{i}^{\mu_{l}}\eta^{\rho_{l}}_{~~\gamma_{i}}\eta^{\sigma_{l}}_{~~\gamma_{j}}-p_{i}^{\rho_{l}}\eta^{\mu_{l}}_{~~\gamma_{i}}\eta^{\sigma_{l}}_{~~\gamma_{j}}\Big)\end{aligned}

5)
Derivatives
D^{\alpha_{m}}_{(l)}
p_{i}
\gamma_{i}
p_{j}
\gamma_{j}
\begin{aligned} \hskip 9.24994pt\sim\hskip 9.24994ptD^{\alpha_{m}}_{(l)}=\left(-\frac{i}{2}\right)\sum_{i=0}^{2N+1}\sigma^{(l)}_{i}{\nabla}^{\alpha_{m}}_{i}\end{aligned}
If the line of
p_{i}
pointing to the box
l

then
\sigma_{i}^{(l)}=1
, otherwise
\sigma_{i}^{(l)}=-1
.
It should be put on the left-hand side of all lines and vertices.

6)
Momentum conservation
After evaluating the “
D
” derivatives, replace
p_{i}
by
k
if the line
of
p_{i}
points to the bottom end of the green box, otherwise
p_{i}=\overline{k}
.

7)
Integrate over all the
\lambda
\int^{1}_{0}d\lambda_{1}\int^{\lambda_{1}}_{0}d\lambda_{2}\cdots\int^{\lambda_{n-1}}_{0}d\lambda_{n}

\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\\
=&\delta(k^{2}-m^{2})\epsilon^{\gamma_{0}}_{s}(k)\epsilon^{\gamma_{3*}}_{r}(k)\int_{0}^{1}d\lambda_{1}\int_{0}^{\lambda_{1}}d\lambda_{2}\,\Bigg\{D^{\alpha_{1}}_{(1)}\,D^{\alpha_{2}}_{(2)}\,\frac{2E_{\bm{k}}}{2E_{{\bm{p}}_{1}}2E_{{\bm{p}}_{(2N)}}}t^{\mu_{1}\nu_{1}}_{(1)~\gamma_{1}\gamma_{2}}(p_{1},p_{2})\\
&\times t^{\mu_{2}\nu_{2}}_{(2)~\gamma_{3}\gamma_{4}}(-p_{3},-p_{4})(2{\hat{t}}\cdot p_{1})[n_{B}(\beta\cdot p_{1})+1)](2{\hat{t}}\cdot p_{2})[n_{B}(\beta\cdot p_{2})+1](2{\hat{t}}\cdot p_{4})n_{B}(\beta\cdot p_{4})\\
&\times\left(-\eta^{\gamma_{0}\gamma_{1}}+\frac{p_{1}^{\gamma_{0}}p_{1}^{\gamma_{1}}}{m^{2}}\right)\left(-\eta^{\gamma_{2}\gamma_{3}}+\frac{p_{2}^{\gamma_{2}}p_{2}^{\gamma_{3}}}{m^{2}}\right)\left(-\eta^{\gamma_{4}\gamma_{5}}+\frac{p_{4}^{\gamma_{4}}p_{4}^{\gamma_{5}}}{m^{2}}\right)\Bigg\}\bigg|^{p_{1}=k,p_{2}=\overline{k}}_{p_{3}=\overline{k},p_{4}=k}\end{split}

(82)

with

D^{\alpha_{1}}_{(1)}=\left(-\frac{i}{2}\right)\left(\eta^{\alpha_{1}\zeta_{1}}-\hat{t}^{\alpha_{1}}{\hat{t}}^{\zeta_{1}}\right)\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial p_{1}^{\zeta_{1}}}+\frac{\partial}{\partial p_{2}^{\zeta_{1}}}+\frac{\partial}{\partial p_{3}^{\zeta_{1}}}-\frac{\partial}{\partial p_{4}^{\zeta_{1}}}\right)

(83)

and

\begin{split}D^{\alpha_{2}}_{(2)}&=\left(-\frac{i}{2}\right)\left(\eta^{\alpha_{2}\zeta_{2}}-\hat{t}^{\alpha_{2}}{\hat{t}}^{\zeta_{2}}\right)\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial p_{1}^{\zeta_{2}}}-\frac{\partial}{\partial p_{2}^{\zeta_{2}}}-\frac{\partial}{\partial p_{3}^{\zeta_{2}}}-\frac{\partial}{\partial p_{4}^{\zeta_{2}}}\right).\end{split}

(84)

So far, we have set all momenta to be on the normal mass shell
p_{i}^{\mu}=(E_{{\bm{p}}_{i}},{\bm{p}}_{i})
.
In practice, we can relax these restrictions by replacing
E_{{\bm{p}}_{i}}
by an energy
p_{i}^{0}
,
which is treated as being independent of
{\bm{p}}_{i}
during the calculation, for Feynman rules of the MVSD, lines, and vertices.
The related “
D
”s should be modified to the following form

D^{\alpha_{m}}_{(l)}=\left(-\frac{i}{2}\right)\sum_{i=0}^{2N+1}\sigma^{(l)}_{i}\left[\partial^{\alpha_{m}}_{i}-\hat{p}_{i}^{\alpha_{m}}\left({\hat{t}}\cdot\partial_{i}\right)\right]\,.

(85)

After evaluating the “
D
” derivatives, we still need to assign
p_{i}=k
or
\overline{k}
,
with
k^{\mu}=(E_{\bm{k}},{\bm{k}})
and
\overline{k}^{\mu}=(E_{\bm{k}},-{\bm{k}})
on the mass shell.

(91)

Note that for
f^{(2)}_{rs}|_{TT},\,f^{(2)}_{rs}|_{TS},\,f^{(2)}_{rs}|_{ST},\,f^{(2)}_{rs}|_{SS}
, they contain 16 diagrams each

.

One can utilize FeynCalc
Mertig:1990an
;
Shtabovenko:2016sxi
;
Shtabovenko:2020gxv
to evaluate the diagrams.
The final results are

\begin{split}f_{rs}^{(2)}|_{S}(x,k)=&\delta(k^{2}-m^{2})\theta(k^{0})n_{B}(1+n_{B})\epsilon^{r*}_{(\gamma_{3}}(k)\epsilon^{s}_{\gamma_{0})}(k)\frac{1}{4E_{k}}[\partial^{\perp}_{\alpha_{1}}\Omega_{\rho_{1}}^{\ \,\gamma_{0}}(x)]\\
&\times\left[\hat{k}^{\alpha_{1}}{\hat{t}}^{\rho_{1}}{{\hat{t}}}^{\gamma_{3}}-\gamma_{k}^{2}\,\hat{k}^{\rho_{1}}\eta^{\gamma_{3}\alpha_{1}}-\left(\eta^{\alpha_{1}\rho_{1}}-\frac{k^{\alpha_{1}}k^{\rho_{1}}}{m^{2}}\right){{\hat{t}}}{{}^{\gamma_{3}}}\right]\,,\end{split}

(92)

\begin{split}f_{rs}^{(2)}|_{T}(x,k)&=\delta(k^{2}-m^{2})\theta(k^{0})n_{B}(1+n_{B})\epsilon^{r*}_{(\gamma_{3}}(k)\epsilon^{s}_{\gamma_{0})}(k)\frac{1}{4E_{k}}[\partial^{\perp}_{\alpha_{1}}\partial^{\perp}_{\alpha_{2}}\beta_{\nu_{1}}(x)]\\
&\times\Big[(2{\hat{t}}^{\nu_{1}}+\gamma_{k}^{2}\hat{k}^{\nu_{1}})\eta^{\alpha_{1}\gamma_{0}}\eta^{\gamma_{3}\alpha_{2}}+(2\hat{k}^{\alpha_{1}}\eta^{\alpha_{2}\nu_{1}}+\gamma_{k}^{2}\hat{k}^{\alpha_{1}}\hat{k}^{\alpha_{2}}\hat{k}^{\nu_{1}}){\hat{t}}^{\gamma_{0}}{\hat{t}}^{\gamma_{3}}\\
&-2(\eta^{\alpha_{1}\nu_{1}}+\hat{k}^{\alpha_{1}}{\hat{t}}^{\nu_{1}}+\gamma_{k}^{2}\hat{k}^{\alpha_{1}}\hat{k}^{\nu_{1}})\eta^{\alpha_{2}\gamma_{0}}{\hat{t}}^{\gamma_{3}}\Big]\\
&+\delta(k^{2}-m^{2})\theta(k^{0})n_{B}(1+n_{B})\delta_{rs}\frac{1}{2E_{k}}[\partial^{\perp}_{\alpha_{1}}\partial^{\perp}_{\alpha_{2}}\beta_{\nu_{1}}(x)]\\
&\times\frac{1}{2}\Big\{\eta^{\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}}({\hat{t}}^{\nu_{1}}+\hat{k}^{\nu_{1}})+\hat{k}^{\alpha_{1}}(2\eta^{\alpha_{2}\nu_{1}}-\hat{k}^{\alpha_{2}}{\hat{t}}^{\nu_{1}}+3\hat{k}^{\alpha_{2}}\hat{k}^{\nu_{1}})\\
&+(2n_{B}+1){\gamma_{k}}({\hat{t}}\cdot\tilde{\beta})(\eta^{\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}}+\hat{k}^{\alpha_{1}}\hat{k}^{\alpha_{2}})\hat{k}^{\nu_{1}}+\frac{1}{3}{\gamma_{k}}^{2}[6n_{B}(1+n_{B})+1]\hat{k}^{\nu_{1}}\\
&\times\left[\tilde{\beta}^{\alpha_{1}}\tilde{\beta}^{\alpha_{2}}+({\hat{t}}\cdot\tilde{\beta})\hat{k}^{\alpha_{1}}\hat{k}^{\alpha_{2}}-2({\hat{t}}\cdot\tilde{\beta})\tilde{\beta}^{\alpha_{1}}\hat{k}^{\alpha_{2}}\right]\Big\}\,,\end{split}

(93)
